Katalog Berita (Halaman 46/58)

#2456. Aspect analysis: Leipzig never found a trigger – LC

von Next Generation am 20.10.2026 in den Kategorien Uncategorized mit 0 Kommentaren

A 6-1 reads like a game in which everything fell apart. Look at how the goals came about one by one and a different picture emerges: it was not six separate problems but two or three, which Bayern punished in seven variations. No pressure on the man in possession, marking problems in central areas, and a back line that did not work with the movements in front of it – that chain runs through practically every goal conceded.

This aspect analysis therefore takes the goals rather than the game as its material and works back from there to the patterns.

Bayern triggered very high throughout, stayed tight to their opponents and pushed through centrally without hesitation. Vandevoordt was pressed from Bayern’s right, with the clear intention of keeping him away from the left-hand build-up route and forcing the long ball. Leipzig’s front line of Nusa and Brajan Gruda repeatedly angled their runs deep against Nathaniel Brown and Konrad Laimer.

Bayern had one extra central player dropping into midfield in this phase, usually Kimmich. Had Leipzig played short to the right, he could have pressed through onto Raum; in practice he stayed central and pushed through from there. That left Leipzig with the long ball, and it was ineffective: it produced almost no regains, so the enforced forward play brought neither relief nor territory.

Going the other way, Leipzig got no access at all, and the reason was Bayern’s rotations. Kimmich dropped right, Pavlović left into a back three, with Upamecano holding a very wide position. Against this shifting three-man build-up Leipzig never found a trigger, because the assignments reset with every rotation – whoever was supposed to step out of midfield first had to work out who he was picking up. The handover of pressing duties out of central areas failed repeatedly.

In front of that, Bayern staggered their shape so that Leipzig’s compactness became the problem. Olise moved into the ten space at times, Nathaniel Brown operated in the half-lane and ran beyond from there, Luis Díaz stayed wide left. Saibari and Kane pinned several opponents centrally while regularly pulling out of the central lane. Because Leipzig pressed very narrow, this produced exactly what Bayern were looking for: once the opponent was committed near the ball, the switch followed, and on the now open side stood a one-on-one.

That already builds the bridge to the goals. The isolated one-on-ones in which Luis Díaz got his three finishes were not individual moments but the product of this sequence of pinning and switching.

1-0, Luis Díaz (12.) – The first goal already contains the full diagnosis. Kimmich receives centrally without any pressure and can turn out with an open body shape. Ahead of him Saibari and Kane occupy the central zone in front of the box, which forces Orban and Lukeba to step out – so far, the intended part. The full-backs, however, do not push up with that movement and stay too deep, while Raum’s position plays the runner onside. Klostermann stays outside and leaves the inside lane unsecured, and that is exactly where Luis Díaz starts from. Out of that unprotected lane Díaz runs into the back line and Kimmich chips the ball in behind. Not one bad decision, but four players whose decisions did not fit together.

2-0, Aleksandar Pavlović (26.) – Same cause, different route. Kane drops deep and Leipzig do not shift across as a unit – the reactions showed it clearly: arms thrown up, gesturing, hands dropping again, the beginnings of frustration. Kane gets the free body shape, and with him the consequence is predictable: the long ball, high or flat. Because Leipzig are so narrow centrally, the ball flies straight through the entire zone in front of their back line. Luis Díaz moves actively into the passing lane to control it while Klostermann tries to get into his running line and does not make it – Díaz reaches the ball first. From the one-on-one he wins, Díaz dribbles centrally at Orban, which creates a four-against-three on the break. Díaz pins Orban and Lukeba centrally, Pavlović overlaps on the left after holding a high, left-leaning position all evening and threatening the back line. Two touches, left foot, finished away from the defender.

3-0, Michael Olise (34.) – Here Bayern’s press shows itself at its clearest. From a Leipzig throw-in, the man-orientation is fully visible: consistent pushing through, clean handovers. A misplaced pass from Raum then cannot be defended, because neither the tracking nor the duelling is good enough to disturb the pass. Kane can open up half-way. Centrally, Olise comes inside from the wing, and Banzuzi and his partner fail to pick him up, letting him run through – against a player of that variability, an invitation to the key pass. Luis Díaz runs beyond and drags Orban with him, Klostermann is pinned wide by Saibari and can no longer get back into central areas. Olise finishes against the goalkeeper’s movement, across his standing leg into the far corner, catching Vandevoordt completely on the wrong foot.

4-0, Luis Díaz (47.) – After a regain, Bayern switch quickly to the far side and once diagonally through, putting Díaz in a one-on-one again. He holds the ball in the dribble, and Leipzig fail to solve the situation in three steps: the centre is not closed, Orban is too late stepping out, Klostermann does not get into the duel. That Vandevoordt leaves the far corner open is the final piece of an isolated one-on-one that should never have arisen.

5-0, Harry Kane (53.) – Structurally the most interesting goal. Leipzig play a long ball without preparing it and react to the consequences contradictorily: the back line does not push up at all, while the midfield line and both forwards counterpress forwards. The ten space opens up enormously, the gaps between the lines become huge – and Olise is standing in that zone. One pass from Kim is enough to take six Leipzig players out of the game. Olise runs unopposed at the back line and lays it off to Díaz, who is in a one-on-one again; Brown joins for the two-against-one and shifts into the inside lane. Klostermann does not pick up his run from the deep half-space, and centrally Kane again drops into Orban’s blind side – a pattern that stood out across the whole game. The result is a two-against-one against the goalkeeper.

6-0, Luis Díaz (69.) – From a set piece Bayern create a five-against-four out wide. Gnabry pins opponents and makes the space for Díaz, Orban again takes one big step in the block and does not get into the duel. The same scene as the 4-0, only from a different phase of play – a sign that this is a behavioural pattern rather than a snapshot.

6-1, Andrija Maksimović (90.+1) – Leipzig’s goal shows in reverse what their own game had been missing. Bayern drop into a deeper block here, and Leipzig combine centrally before going wide. Gruda gets into a one-on-one against Brown, Upamecano steps across – but from above, with too wide a gap and without closing the centre. That opens the one-two, which Leipzig take immediately. Because the whole situation narrows onto that combination, the space behind it stays unoccupied, and Maksimović can take two touches on his left and finish unopposed.

The sequence of goals reads like a repetition. Five of Bayern’s six come from the same combination of no pressure on the ball centrally and a back line that does not manage the space behind it. Luis Díaz stands in an isolated one-on-one three times, Orban is twice too late with a step that is too big, Kane repeatedly drops into his blind side. Klostermann certainly did not have his best day – but it would be too easy to settle the bill with him. In the decisive scenes he faced situations that functioning protection in front of him should never have allowed to arise in the first place.

Behind it all lies not an individual problem but a structural one: Leipzig pressed narrow and found no trigger against Bayern’s rotating back three. That very compactness made the switch of play the opponent’s most effective tool, and at the end of every switch stood a one-on-one that Leipzig lost. The scoreline is not the story – the story is that the same scene worked six times.

LC is often found in a classroom, happiest on a football pitch, and occasionally on LinkedIn.

#2455. If Spanish refs can speak up so can ours... over to you, Willie

Published: 12:39 EST, 13 November 2026 | Updated: 12:46 EST, 13 November 2026

Since the beginning of the month in Spain, referees have started making themselves available for interview after matches to explain decisions and improve transparency.

It has led to people here asking why Scotland’s whistlers aren’t doing the same — and those questions are perfectly valid.

Back in August 2023, leading official John Beaton addressed calls for greater openness around VAR and gave the impression the refereeing firmament were in favour of a new spirit of glasnost.

‘Anything to explain how we are arriving at decisions and anything we can do to show people what we do on a Saturday and what happens in the VAR centre, we absolutely welcome,’ he said.

‘The more transparency, the better. The more questions we get, the more answers we can provide and, we hope, get to a better place,’

That was followed by new head of refereeing Willie Collum’s VAR show on YouTube the following season. At that point, there seemed a real move to lift the lid and build confidence in our men in black. There was talk of Collum doing his programme more than once a month and suggestions referees might even be open to being interviewed post-match in the right circumstances.

Unfortunately, though, all that has come to nothing and last term — in which controversial decisions had a huge effect on the title race — feels like it could become an obstacle in the way of progress.

Head of Referees Willie Collum should be applauded for speaking up on the SFA VAR show

It needn’t be, though. There has always been a feeling among referees that being put in front of the cameras carried danger. SFA Ian Maxwell has also spoken about the game being subject to ‘fairly hysterical commentary’ when something bad happens.

The media environment here is nowhere near as febrile as in Spain, though. It has four newspapers dedicated to sport alone. There are countless chat shows on council telly full of talking heads. Ages ago, news bulletins there had lipreaders telling viewers what coaches and players were saying on the park. Jose Mourinho is also back ruffling officials’ feathers at Real Madrid.

Getting Spanish refs to a place where they feel ready to be interviewed has taken time. It has also taken extra cash for them from TV money and it can still go a lot further.

A selected referee will speak to broadcast rights-holders only. And it’s not after every game. Only selected fixtures.

The SFA made real progress a couple of years back in lifting the curtain and should seek to rediscover that momentum — even though it won’t make the key issue of having to improve overall standards go away.

Since last year, the Spanish FA have been broadcasting their own YouTube show ‘Tiempo de Revision’ to address flashpoints. It should be said that it’s inferior to Collum’s broadcast in terms of content.

It replays incidents against a musical backdrop with captions explaining the decisions. There is no in-game audio, no one like Collum putting himself out there.

Although some of his reasoning for big calls was a bit suspect last term, that programme remains a platform to build from.

There is no reason why referees shouldn’t explain their decision-making after games here. Criticism they may or may not receive in the aftermath shouldn’t come into it.

If Spanish whistlers can go public, so can they.

#2454. Access, Not Chances: How Como Took Manchester United Apart – LC

von Next Generation am 24.10.2026 in den Kategorien Champions League,Uncategorized mit 0 Kommentaren

Como beat Manchester United 4-1 — and in this case the scoreline really is a fair expression of the game. For long stretches the match was one-sided, not in terms of chance volume but in terms of access: Como dictated in every phase where the ball went, who received it, and under what conditions it had to be played on. United never got on terms in any of those phases.

Cesc Fàbregas’s side showed two things that rarely go together. Out of possession, a pressing structure with clear direction, isolation of one side, and man-marking that applied near the ball only for the action in progress while everything around it was already speculating on the second ball. And in possession, a positional game of remarkable technical maturity that ties opponents in, switches play, and works systematically into the space behind them. The 4-1 is therefore less an outlier than evidence of work that has been built up over recent seasons.

This analysis goes through the phases one by one: first Como in possession and United’s response, then the work against the ball from the high press to defending the box, and finally the goals.

In build-up, Como moved away from their nominal 4-2-3-1 and sat closer to a 4-3-3, with the eights as the real variable: very free in their movement and above all active in the half-spaces. The base structure stayed stable — a flat four plus a six in front of them, sometimes ahead of the line, sometimes slightly behind the forward line.

Close to their own goal, Como consistently involved goalkeeper Butez as an additional central player in the build-up. That created a five-against-four against United’s front line — awkward on paper, more awkward still in execution, because Como didn’t use the overload to play through but to tie opponents in. From there, the same shape kept appearing on both the right and the left: centre-back, full-back, a player from the half-position in the eight space, and a six alongside. A diamond that wasn’t an end in itself but had a purpose: the diagonal route out.

Como’s build-up structure. From a 4-1 (flat four), the half-positions with Paz and Perrone are added. Small triangles and diamonds emerge. The three attackers ahead of them create additional options in behind.

One detail deserves particular attention. At times the full-backs stood not wide but flat, in a half-position. That sounds like a nuance, but it had a clear function: it vacated the wing and created the space the eights could drop into — Paz and Perrone did so regularly. If you don’t occupy the wing yourself, you force the opponent to guard it anyway.

ManU got no access near the ball and left large diagonal spaces open on the far side.

For United’s midfielders this was close to unsolvable. Tielemans, Mount and Andrey Santos spent the whole game struggling to establish any responsibility at all, let alone get into duels. When an eight dropped out, they couldn’t follow, and if one of them was tied in by it, a two-against-one opened up on the far wing. Como didn’t create that situation once; they created it serially.

The decisive mechanism lay in receiving and relaying the ball. The player in the deeper position usually worked with two touches: the first took the ball backwards, the second relayed it to the free teammate. At the end of every one of those actions stood a player with the game in front of him — and from that open position he could switch play.

What made it possible were the preparatory movements. Paz and captain Da Cunha first lured their markers in the wrong direction and then worked with the head start they’d bought. That head start isn’t only space, it’s above all time: time to control the ball first and relay it second, rather than having to do both in one touch.

A scene in the 17th minute is typical. Mason Mount steps out, Mazraoui goes into the duel at the same time — nominally United are in an overload here. Only neither of them really gets to the opponent, because both are too late. Paz can therefore calmly take his touch backwards and switch play directly with the second, dissolving the pressure before it had even formed.

When the build-up ran down the right, it showed in a recurring pattern: Paz and Da Cunha took the first touch backwards or inside and then switched with the left foot out of a closed body position. The players they found were systematically in open positions — Milla, the far-side six, or the wing-to-wing connection directly.

In the second half, Como adjusted the shape and more often played with two sixes, to form the diamonds faster and switch to the far-side six faster. Couto stood out in this: the right-back repeatedly played the chip into the six or ten space — six or seven times in a row during one spell. It was mainly Paz who received the ball there and could launch the attack.

In the second half Como focus on those diagonal spaces and look for them mainly with chipped balls.

Especially striking inside the diamond are the rotations between the players. Because of the connections between them, they moved in rotation depending on running path, space and ball movement. The same recurring spaces were re-occupied. Under pressure on the ball carrier, the outer players pushed flat or ran in behind the opponents; the highest player in the diamond burst out of the cover shadow and became the reference point in depth. On a deep pass, the two wide players released and joined the depth, able to offer themselves again immediately in an open body position. If the pass went via the flat wide players, the spaces in front of the new ball carrier could be run into. Every rotation, every positional adjustment was coordinated with the others and, in the end, produced a stream of pressure-resolving passes while Manchester United chased the press in vain.

Como bridged the middle third exceptionally quickly, via dribbles at speed and diagonal passes. Behind it stood a recognisable principle: first tie the opponent to one wing, then get behind them via the diagonal, and in the final third do the same thing again. In sum, that produced the shape of a Z — build-up on the right, transition on the left, finish from the right again.

In this example Como pin ManU in the press with their short passing. Thanks to the extra attacker on the ball-near side they can play through the pressure and switch.

The connections between wingers and striker carried these attacks and were deliberately kept flexible. The wide players sometimes stayed wide, sometimes came short, sometimes tucked inside. Douvikas accordingly either waited deep in front of the line for the chipped ball or, when space opened up on the wing, received the deeper pass and then ran in behind along the wing as soon as the ball sat on the full-back’s open foot. That flexibility produced the overloads on one side, and the overloads produced the central overload.

In the final third one element was added that is rare in fast transition teams: patience. Como drew opponents towards them with dribbles and short passes, used back-passes when needed, and then switched in front of the block. Da Cunha, Baturina and Paz were the three who could tie opponents in and then find the diagonal. They created the positions on the diagonal, were available to receive, and used their space to apply pressure. The block was usually tied in with short touches, the switch and the position behind the opponent prepared, and quick finishes sought.

The intensity in possession shouldn’t be underestimated. Practically every attack involved at least three or four players, the width was occupied, the centre likewise, and there were multiple options throughout. What they looked for almost every time was the space behind the opponent on the far side — exactly the zone that is closed last when a block shifts across.

Paz brought the right profile for this pattern. A strong left foot cutting inside from the right, he repeatedly dropped into the right-hand blind side of his opponents, got shots away there with few touches, and racked up the most shots of the match. That the finishes generally came off few touches isn’t a detail but the consequence of the time won further back.

As the move continues, Como again look for the blind side of the defenders. Here Douvikas and Paz are positioned in behind, with Da Cunha joining in front of the back line. After briefly pinning the opponents, the switch reaches Da Cunha, who misses the target from distance.

What carries all of this is the technical execution. Soft passes, sharp ones when the moment called for it, consistently well timed and played into the right foot — for ninety minutes. Passing that secure at that tempo is what makes a variable concept actually retrievable.

United’s basic problem out of possession was the hybrid. They pressed half man-oriented, but with a plus-one in the last line — a construction that forces compromises in both directions. It demands access up front that it doesn’t fund at the back.

There were certainly moments when it worked. When Dalot pushed through into the press on the right and passed his man backwards, a clean man-orientation emerged all the way up to the front — and with it a clear assignment for Mount, out of which the ball could be won. But that stayed the exception. Most of the time Dalot sat too flat out of possession, so United couldn’t trigger the duels up front and went into the press a man short.

Mount was the most conspicuous case in this respect. He barely got into a duel across the whole match because the decision to step out always came too late. Against a team that works with preparatory movements, a late decision is practically the same as none: the opponent already has the head start before the journey even begins.

On top of that came a problem on the far side. There, United’s winger frequently failed to protect the depth — usually Mbeumo, who jogged back rather than closing the space. But that was precisely the target zone of Como’s diagonal switches. It allowed them to change sides almost at will and regularly find an open structure on the other side.

The second half brought no improvement. The distances to opponents stayed large, and centrally what was actually intended still didn’t materialise. Michael Carrick’s double change at half-time was meant to bring more physicality to the six with Carlos Baleba, but it didn’t solve the problem — the assignments still didn’t work, the duels in the centre still didn’t happen, and the wide positions dragged the eights out further, which opened new spaces.

In the end the energy simply wasn’t there to press with the necessary intensity. United’s press became increasingly static, and a static press against an opponent with this passing quality yields not a single ball win — it only hands them the starting positions they’re looking for anyway.

Como started the game in a high press and sustained it above all in the first half, with isolated spells after the break too. It was triggered on back-passes and goal kicks — and, remarkably, by their own goals: after every strike the team immediately pushed high again instead of managing the lead in a deeper block.

The steering ran consistently towards United’s left build-up side. The reason is personnel: Harry Maguire played there with his weaker left foot as the outside foot, and next to him stood another right-footer in Noussair Mazraoui on the left. Two right-footers on one build-up side means the ball always has to be taken back inside to play on — exactly where Como were closing the door. The press was usually triggered by the left-sided forward, with the ball ending up at either Maguire or Mazraoui.

Pressing structure after the change in the second half.

In the centre Como defended man-oriented, with both sixes pushing very high and taking flat passes into United’s midfielders out of the game. That removed the short route through the middle as an option and left the long ball. But the long ball triggered nothing for United: the second balls went to Como practically every time.

That isn’t chance, it’s running work. The sixes consistently tracked the negative pressing paths back, anticipated the long ball early, and were in front of the line before it arrived. Luis Milla covered the most ground of any player on the pitch in this pattern — recovery runs after being played past, doubling up on the ball, attacking the second ball. When the long ball came from Maguire or one of the full-backs, a six was regularly in front of the line with an intense sprint, sealing the centre, taking away the diagonal follow-up passes and attacking the loose balls.

Defending the long ball and extra support on the second ball.

Once the ball was on one side, that side was isolated. The near-side forward had access to the centre-back and dropped back a touch when the pass went to the full-back, so that he covered centre-back and six simultaneously and closed the square pass. The far-side forward marked the goalkeeper. That served two functions at once: it took away United’s escape route via the keeper along with the switch, and it kept a player in position for the moment of the turnover.

The double pivot worked in a deliberate half-position. The near-side six stayed tight to his man, the far-side one dropped slightly behind him — securing the space and at the same time establishing access to the far-side centre-back in United’s back three.

The last line defended largely man-for-man in the high press, and very tightly. But the decisive detail is the one that carries the whole concept: the man-marking near the ball applied only to the action in progress. Around it, players were already releasing from their assignments and orienting towards the second ball. That is only retrievable at very high intensity — and it’s the reason United’s forced long balls produced not a single usable moment.

When Como dropped a line, the man-orientation at the second height stayed exceptionally high. Here too the back-pass served as the trigger to push up as a unit and tip back into man-marking. If United were in a closed body position, the Comaschi had already pushed up far enough to attack the back-pass at the moment it was played — a pattern that led directly to the 3-0. The half-position was at work there again: closing down level with the centre-back while simultaneously prepared to push through onto the next player deeper.

In the first half Como still had problems assigning the half-positions against ManU’s 3-2-5. In this example Fernandes moves to the wing, Como fail to establish responsibility and access and are played through at mid-height.

Up to half-time, though, Como had a structural problem with this. The pure man-marking didn’t come off cleanly, because of the two sixes in front of the line, one should really have tucked in and taken over the half-position for the centre-backs. That didn’t happen consistently. The consequence: a centre-back stepped out, the space behind him stayed unoccupied, and that was exactly what United kept attacking.

The pattern of pressing onto Manchester United’s left side continued in the second half, now with Dalot at full-back. The press was still triggered by Douvikas, with the ball-near winger pushing up as a second striker to press the centre-back. Paz focused on the visitors’ ball-near six and Couto pushed through onto Dalot. Through the central connections the game was isolated on one side.

At half-time Como adjusted. One six moved directly into the line, with the far-side winger pushing in onto the six — they now pressed out of a 5-2-1-2. Lucas Da Cunha pressed through from the right onto Maguire, while Nico Paz picked up United’s six. The central forward pressed the central centre-back and from there carried on to the goalkeeper. The far-side wide midfielder and the remaining six were poised — one onto United’s free six, one onto the far-side centre-back — again in that half-position between opponent and the space to be protected.

ManU building up high against the man-oriented Italians.

The effect was immediately visible. Como controlled the half-positions, got access right into the last line throughout, and set the rhythm of the match from then on. The adjustment solved exactly the problem the first half had left open, without changing the underlying principle.

In the final phase Como defended from a very deep line inside the box. That left barely any space behind the line to play into — which United hardly looked for anyway. The visitors attacked little depth and rarely occupied the width in the final third, so Como could stay compact without being punished for it.

Accordingly, a lot of players stood very centrally in and in front of the box. That produced numerous cover shadows and a numerical superiority around the ball, which allowed them to steer actively within the block rather than merely react. At times they deliberately steered inside, to guide the opponent into the prepared congestion and cover from a deeper position.

Deep back line in the box, centre closed and play steered inside. The box defending allowed the English side shots from distance.

The crucial point is that Como left the gaps open not despite this set-up but because of it. If you pack the box and secure the depth, you give up the space in front of the line — and that is exactly where United were supposed to shoot from.

The numbers vindicate the decision. Taking the conceded goal out of the box, Como allowed nine shots from outside the penalty area across ninety minutes, worth roughly 0.24 xG combined; only three of them were even on target. On top of that, of the four shots inside the box, two came from at least 14 to 15 metres and effectively belong in the long-range category as well. United accepted the invitation, and the shooting quality was perfectly decent — it’s just that with that scoring probability over a whole match, the most likely outcome is precisely what happened.

1-0, Nico Paz (2′) — The goal comes before United are even set. Como take the goal kick quickly and play straight to the full-back, luring the visitors into the press — only not cleanly. Mason Mount triggers the pressure and is played past, and on the second ball he goes into the duel as well instead of holding the middle and looking for the space where he could pick the ball up. That is exactly what opens the centre, the space for the second ball is there, and Paz can play it through.

What follows is the transition principle in its purest form: a quick switch to the diagonal side, then short passes against a very deep line. Matheus Cunha doesn’t pick up the player behind him and leaves the ten space open, where Paz is standing. He needs two touches because neither Cunha from above nor Maguire from his distance gets into the duel. Lammens, for his part, doesn’t defend the goal with his cover shadow but takes the near post — which leaves the far corner to a strong left foot. The curled finish into it is quality, though.

2-0, Anastasios Douvikas (49′, assist Paz) — Same zone, same cause. Bryan Mbeumo doesn’t choose to shut the centre but closes the centre-back, which creates the space for Da Cunha, who drives forward and plays Martin Baturina in. Centrally, Mount again fails to pick up his man. Out wide, Amad Diallo, introduced at half-time, generates no pressure centrally, so Mazraoui can’t take over the deep wide player in the first place.

The result is Paz behind the opponent, in the ten space, dribbling unopposed at goal. Matthijs de Ligt establishes no reference to Douvikas’s run in behind and slips as well — a one-against-one with the goalkeeper that Douvikas finishes coolly.

3-0, Nico Paz (57′) — The only goal that comes directly out of the work against the ball. Como are in the midfield press in a closed position with access to the inside lane, win the ball there and punish a lapse of concentration in the visitors’ build-up. At the end, Paz is alone in front of Lammens again.

4-0, Jacobo Ramón (76′, assist Kaiki) — This goal comes from a longer possession spell and shows the assignment problem in its clearest form. Mazraoui is too narrow and has no access to the wide player. Diallo stands in the space with access to neither the central player nor the full-back nor the winger — an orientation that doesn’t work in any direction.

Como switch to Máximo Perrone, who can slide the ball out wide because the space is simply free. From there comes the cross from an open body position, and in the centre Como run a two-against-one at the end of which Ramón scores.

4-1, Joshua Zirkzee (90′) — Como’s only lapse, and one in a phase in which the team had already dropped very deep. United get the ball to the wing, it’s two against two in the box, and the cross is initially only feinted. Zirkzee orients himself well forwards at that moment, while Como’s defenders are already stepping out after the feinted cross. Diallo’s ball in behind the line is strong, the offside call is razor-thin in his favour, and Zirkzee takes two touches before finishing one-against-one with the goalkeeper. A blemish, not a structural problem.

The common denominator — Three of Como’s four goals come in the same zone: the space in front of and behind United’s line that nobody takes responsibility for. Mount is involved in two of those scenes through an assignment he never establishes, Diallo in two more after coming on. Nico Paz is at the heart of three of the four goals, twice as scorer, once as the provider — always out of the opponent’s blind side.

Como’s 4-1 has one common cause across every phase, and it lies less in any single idea than in its execution under continuous load. Out of possession they steered, isolated and speculated on the second ball — with man-marking that applied near the ball only for the action in progress and was already dissolved around it. In possession they tied opponents in, switched play and worked systematically into the space behind. Both require eleven players to anticipate a teammate’s decision, and both functioned over the full distance.

The connecting detail is the half-position. In the press, the far-side six stood between opponent and the space to be protected; in possession, the eights worked out of the same zone, and the full-backs occupied it flat in order to vacate the wing for exactly those players. Como, in other words, occupied the position in both directions from which two jobs can be done at once — which is why the side could neither be played past in the press nor be pinned in the build-up.

For United the diagnosis is more uncomfortable than the result. The problem wasn’t individual errors but that the team never, in any phase, got into the duels it needed. The mixture of half man-orientation and a plus-one in the last line funded no access up front, and without access every decision is made too late — from Mount in the centre to Mbeumo failing to secure the far-side depth. Carrick’s double change at half-time was meant to bring physicality to the six with Carlos Baleba, but it changed nothing about the assignments, and in Diallo a player came on wide who reached neither one opponent nor the other in his space. That the energy ran out in the end was a consequence rather than a cause.

That leaves the context. A 4-1 against Manchester United is no proof in itself, and Como’s finishing was exceptionally efficient on the night. What showed regardless of the result, though, is a team with a recognisable, rehearsed answer in all four phases of the game — and one that put a technical maturity on the pitch in possession that you wouldn’t readily credit to a side promoted two years ago.

#2453. Les Vertes sont mieux que des Nice Girls

Les Amazones poursuivent leur sans-faute en Arkema Seconde Ligue !Ce samedi, les protégées de Yannick Chandioux ont décroché leur sixième succès consécutif en dominant l'OGC Nice (1-0) au stade Salif-Keïta. Avant cette rencontre, les Stéphanoises occupaient la première place du classement avec 17 points, devant Le Mans et Roubaix, tandis que les Azuréennes pointaient au dixième rang seulement.Pour conserver son fauteuil de leader, le coach ligérien avait reconduit Ithaisa Viñoly dans les cages, derrière une défense Couasnon-Kack-Blanchard-Sierra. Faustine Bataillard et Héloïse Mansuy étaient chargées de l'entrejeu, tandis que la capitaine Sarah Cambot accompagnait Jana Zufankova, Tyler Isgrig et Dona Scannapieco dans le secteur offensif.Et les Vertes ne tardaient pas à prendre les choses en main malgré une première (et la seule) intervention d'Ithaisa Viñoly. D'abord une première tentative de Jana Zufankova dès la 2e minute puis une ouverture du score logique au quart d'heure de jeu: sur un centre parfaitement ajusté de Sarah Cambot, Tyler Isgrig surgissait au second poteau pour placer une tête victorieuse et inscrire son 5e pion de la saison, le 4e enc championnat (1-0, 15e).Les Amazones manquaient même le break dans la foulée lorsque la tentative de Mansuy était sauvée sur la ligne (20e) puis celle de Cambot repoussée par la gardienne niçoise (38e).Avec leur petit avantage; les Vertes continuaient de monopoliser le ballon en deuxième période sans parvenir à tuer le suspense. Ninon Blanchard trouvait notamment la transversale sur corner (65e) puis Dona Scannapieco butait à son tour sur l'excellente portière azuréenne (75e). Malgré quelques timides velléités niçoises en fin de rencontre, la défense stéphanoise tenait bon pour préserver ce court mais précieux avantage.

Yannick Chandioux: "Je suis satisfait de cette victoire, notamment de notre première période, durant laquelle nous nous sommes procuré beaucoup d’occasions (...) En revanche, il n’est pas normal de ne pas avoir réussi à creuser l’écart avec une vingtaine d’occasions et plus d’une dizaine de corners. Nous avons encore du travail à faire dans ce domaine. Il faut forcément souligner cette série de six victoires consécutives en championnat. Nous avons également signé un nouveau clean-sheet, tout en nous montrant dangereux offensivement. Cette série doit surtout nous rappeler que nous devons rester pleinement concernés. J’essaie d’impliquer l’ensemble de mon groupe dans cette dynamique: chacune a eu et aura sa chance"

En effet, avec désormais 20 points au compteur, six victoires consécutives et toujours aucune défaite après huit journées, les Vertes confirment leur place de leader, uniquement talonnées par Le Mans et Roubaix, formations toutes deux victorieuses le lendemain. De quoi aborder la suite avec confiance, à commencer par un déplacement à Guéret, pensionnaire de Régional 2, dimanche prochain pour leur entrée en lice en Coupe de France. L'occasion pour les Stéphanoises de poursuivre leur belle dynamique et, pourquoi pas, de s'offrir un joli parcours dans la compétition !

#2447. Boston was a lovely holiday romance, but let's leave it there. Talk of taking Tartan Army back there every two years is just cringeworthy

Published: 11:18 EST, 13 November 2026 | Updated: 13:03 EST, 13 November 2026

Boston was the loveliest little holiday romance. An unforgettable moment in time. But let’s just leave it at that, shall we?

Can’t we just allow those involved in the shenanigans around the World Cup last summer to reflect misty-eyed on doorways to Xanadu being opened by the tilt of the kilt and maybe even the heady nights they can’t remember much about at all?

Or does everything have to be milked to the max these days? Flogged like a dead horse to benefit someone or other. Ruined, really. Played out until every scintilla of fun has been drained out of it.

A bit like Yes, Sir, I Can Boogie. That started out as a bona-fide hoot. A half-cut Andy Considine dressing up in drag with his friends and family during his stag do in London in 2015 and making a music video around Baccara’s 1977 hit. It went viral. Obviously. It was – and is – hilarious.

When Scotland’s players resurrected it in the dressing room in Serbia five years later – with Considine as part of the squad – after the national team had ended a 23-year wait to make it to a major competition, it was simply inspired.

Every second of those infectious scenes exuded elation, joy, togetherness and pin-sharp humour, a killer recipe for the interludes that make life and living so great.

Now? Even the opening bars of the song bring on a sense of dread. It was splattered over T-shirts, played everywhere on repeat and still gets aired at every Scotland home game.

The Tartan Army march to Boston's Fenway Park baseball stadium

It’s boring. It’s been done to death and now lives firmly in that grim, stagnant swamp known as Scottish mainstream culture along with munchy boxes, Bitz N Pieces, theatre shows about the Old Firm and ex-footballers suddenly developing a personality and getting involved in stage shows to tell the world how Wee Dazza once did Big Jazza a beauty in the dressing-room by leaving a squelchy banana in his shoe. The banter anawrat.

Everything ends up spoiled here and it looks like we’re already well down that road with the Tartan Army thanks to the news breaking that the Scottish FA are in talks with the State of Massachusetts over a biennial friendly match at the Gillette Stadium, the black hole amid the festivity at the finals thanks to struggling past Haiti and embarrassing ourselves against Morocco.

State senator Paul Feeney has been across the pond to press the flesh. Massachusetts senate president Karen Spilka is already talking about staging Tartan Day – presumably some rip-off of that twee, shortbread-tin abomination in New York – and ‘other events’.

The heart sinks. Listen, the minute politicians get involved in any kind of ‘happening’, the best thing to do is give it a wide berth.

Mind you, before the Big Swerve, it might be worthwhile having someone remind these characters from The Bay State that Massachusetts’ first contribution to Scotland supporters hitting town in summer involved hiking the cost of the 25-mile train ride from Boston to the ground from $20 to a ludicrous $80.

What unfolded there in mid-June was nothing to do with them. It was everything to do with spontaneity. As it ought to be. A glorious coming-together of all sorts of folk up for a right, royal carry-on.

There were so many glorious vignettes. Scotland punters taking over Red Sox games. Bars running out of beer. Mass marches. Local women, doped-up on Outlander, telling TikTok how a bunch of jakeys intent on drinking anything within arm’s reach – except Bud Light, of course – were making them feel ‘seen’.

The Tartan Army make their presence known in Boston during the summer's World Cup

It was brilliant. A short spell in which stars aligned and magic happened. Boston had everything the Tartan Army required. And going by the testimony of Bostonians, the Tartan Army brought badly needed vitality back to the streets of a city quietened by Covid and other factors.

Everybody won. Everyone there still dines out on the memory. But that’s where it should stay. In the memory. These conversations about trying to recapture that spirit, recreate it every two years around some meaningless friendly against opposition unconfirmed and possible business meetings on the periphery, feel quite sad. How can it have any legs long-term?

Joe and Josephine Punter have moved on. They’re on the batter in other places now. Next week, it’s Switzerland for a Nations League game. After that, it’s wherever the draw for the Euro qualifiers throws them. New cities. New adventures. As football away days ought to be. In, out, shake it all about.

Away days are not about teaming up with suit-wearing dignitaries to be paraded at the town hall, like performing seals in plaid – even though there does appear to be an element of the Scotland support that enjoys that kind of thing.

Away days are not about being used as political capital or forming part of some constructed ‘festival’.

Look, in most aspects of life, it’s generally best not to go back. Sticking traffic cones on top of statues all over town won’t be as funny next time. There won’t be a 20,000-strong travelling support spending their life savings on games an entire generation had been waiting for. There won’t be anything like the same buzz, if any.

Some folk might fancy returning once, just to see if it feels the same. The idea that this is going to be a big deal every two years, though, feels deeply misguided.

Boston’s Irish. Not Scottish. That Tartan Week nonsense in New York is a toe-curlingly awful melange of kilts, Scottie dogs and actors living in America telling everyone back home how to vote.

No self-respecting Scot should give it house room. If there is a plan for the same thing in Boston, the same rules apply.

Like NYC, it’ll suit those from the political class in the mood for a junket, of course. John Swinney will already be counting the air miles if there’s another game in Massachusetts to go to.

The public, of course, is still counting the cost of the First Minister’s last trip there for the World Cup. The bill came in at £45,000 for him and two senior aides to fly business class. The entire trip for government staff came to more than £98,000.

Swinney plays the role with aplomb at these affairs, supping a pint in his Scotland top. The Scottish Football Association need to ask themselves this, though: why should they put themselves at the heart of some kind of cock-eyed project to build bridges politically?

The Tartan Army leave their signature traffic cone in a park in Boston

First up, the players are hardly likely to welcome a pointless week in America being added to a jam-packed schedule. More importantly, though, what exactly has this SNP-led administration, outwith giving a few bob to grassroots, done for Scottish football down the years?

From this vantage point, they’ve been no friend to the national game or its followers. The Offensive Behaviour at Football Act, repealed in 2018, was a scandalous piece of legislation that criminalised fans and made going to football unpleasant.

Clubs have had to fight tooth and nail to secure trials to sell pints inside stadiums when other sports are allowed to do what they like. After trouble in Glasgow city centre ahead of the Old Firm League Cup final two years ago, Swinney stated that ‘the clubs have got a responsibility to make sure their fans behave properly’.

Inside their grounds, yes. What are they meant to do, though, about teenagers running amok on Argyle Street?

Looking after that is the job of the police. Of course, anyone reading 1919 magazine, funded by the Scottish Police Federation, earlier this year will have noted an article about policing around football being ‘chaotic’ and understaffed because of SNP Government budget cuts.

Just like everything else in this sorry little nation built on buying votes for ‘free stuff’, Police Scotland is creaking at the seams. Why would anyone want to get involved in another transatlantic piece of theatre selling some false, outdated, vaguely comical vision of it to foreigners?

A pointless Scotland friendly – served up as part of some forced, hackneyed ‘cultural exchange’ or not – is hardly an idea to be getting on board with.

Pure dead brilliant, it won’t be. More like pure cringe.

#2440. On a déconné avec Da Cunha (22)

Resté un grand supporter des Verts qui l'ont pourtant recalé plusieurs fois à l'adolescence au grand dam de Dominique Rocheteau, Lucas Da Cunha est "le grand gagnant de l'ère Zidane" comme l'a indiqué aujourd'hui Le Dauphiné Libéré.

L'AFP souligne les performances du natif de Roanne (Loire) en équipe de France, jugées "très satisfaisantes" par son sélectionneur. "L'ailier de formation a été placé en meneur de jeu par Cesc Fabregas en Serie A où il s'est révélé après quelques échecs en Ligue 1. Le patron des Bleus l'a encore fait reculer d'un cran en le plaçant dans un rôle de sentinelle où Da Cunha, qui découvrait le poste, a montré beaucoup de promesses et quelques lacunes, en défense notamment. Mais Zidane qui a procédé à une large revue d'effectif lors des deux rassemblements de l'automne, lui a accordé une confiance telle que le capitaine de Côme est le joueur de champ qui compte le plus de temps de jeu à égalité avec Michael Olise (386 minutes chacun)."

Dans sa dernière édition, la Pravda n'est pas en reste. "On dit d'un entraîneur qu'il couche toujours un nom en premier quand il compose son onze de départ. Concernant Zinédine Zidane chez les Bleus, on va finir par penser que ce nom est celui de Lucas Da Cunha, qui avait été le meilleur Français lors de la défaite en ltalie. Contre la Turquie, le meneur de jeu reculé a cherché à accélérer la circulation de balle des Bleus, qui manquait souvent de verticalité, tout en grattant plusieurs ballons au pressing ou grâce à ses anticipations (7 récupérations, record du match côté français). Depuis sa position basse, il est capable de renverser le jeu pour donner de l'air à l'équipe, mais aussi d'offrir de très bons ballons de but, comme il l'a fait deux fois pour Kylian Mbappé en dépassant son poste de sentinelle."

"Déjà un pilier de l'ère Zidane" dixit Le Parisien, le joueur de 25 ans savoure sur TF1. "Les compliments de Zidane sont géniaux, c’est exceptionnel. Il m’a donné beaucoup de responsabilités depuis que je suis arrivé. J’essaie d’appliquer tout ce qu’il me demande, que ce soit sur le plan défensif ou offensif. Je sens que j'ai encore une grosse marge de progression, c'est ça qui est super intéressant pour moi. C’est sûr que l’ascension est folle. Il y a encore quelques années, j’étais ailier. Aujourd’hui, je suis milieu de terrain et je suis là avec les Bleus. Quand on commence le foot, quand on est jeune, c’est un rêve de pouvoir porter ce maillot. On rêve de ça. Et le jour où ça arrive, on n’y croit pas trop. J’adore, j’y prends goût. C’est une grande fierté d’être ici."

#2423. Tanenbaum sondé pour reprendre le Montréal CF ?

Administrateur du site web sportif canadien Dans les coulisses, le rappeur et compositeur Maxime Truman a indiqué jeudi dernier que le milliardaire propriétaire de l'ASSE aurait été sondé pour reprendre le Montreal CF (anciennement dénommé Impact de Montréal), ex-club québécois de Patrice Ferri, Idriss Ech Chergui et Ignacio Piatti. Extraits.

"Geoff Molson aurait eu des discussions avec Larry Tanenbaum afin d'aller voir, ne serait-ce que dans une étape exploratoire, si l'idée d'acheter le CF Montréal (pour le garder à Montréal) a du sens économiquement parlant. Larry Tanenbaum est un Ontarien de 81 ans dont la fortune est évaluée à un peu moins de 3 milliards de dollars américains nets. Il a vendu le mois dernier les parts qu'il détenait dans MLSE pour une société de 4,35 milliards de dollars canadiens, argent qu'il a utilisé pour rembourser des dettes d'investissement et pour sécuriser un peu son patrimoine. Du moins, à court terme…

Il a joué un rôle déterminant dans l'acquisition des Raptors de Toronto et de l'Air Canada Centre, donnant naissance à l'empire MLSE. Il a agi à titre de gouverneur du comité exécutif pour les Maple Leafs, les Raptors, le Toronto FC et les Argonauts. Il est connu, apprécié et respecté des dirigeants et des autres propriétaires de la MLS. Il a même été élu président du conseil des gouverneurs de la NBA. Tanenbaum possède encore plusieurs entités sportives professionnelles, via sa société Kilmer Sports Venture : Tempo de Toronto (avec Geoff Molson, WNBA), LPHF (il a été le premier investisseur canadien un peu plus tôt cette année) et AS Saint-Étienne (Ligue 2 en France). Il possède aussi la Compagnie d'embouteillage Coca-Cola Canada Limitée (Coca-Cola Canada Bottling).

La candidature de Larry Tanenbaum n'est toutefois pas parfaite. First, il est un fier Torontois. Il est membre de l'Ontario Sports Hall of Fame et il a fait des dons majeurs dans la Ville Reine, notamment pour lancer l'Institut Tanenbaum des sciences du sport à l'Université de Toronto. Il a encore une équipe à Toronto (Tempo), ce qui rendrait possiblement un investissement à Montréal « discutable » aux yeux de plusieurs Torontois. Mais bon, Geoff Molson a investi à Toronto (Tempo) et on l'aime encore à Montréal… Second, Tanenbaum a retiré ses billes de MLSE pour les réinvestir dans de plus petits projets. Le Tempo lui a coûté 50 millions de dollars américains il y a deux ans et il a payé l'ASSE environ 25 millions d'euros la même année. Bref, il semble désormais privilégier les investissements un peu plus modestes. Third, à 81 ans, on pourrait penser qu'il ne se lancera pas dans d'énormes investissements plutôt risqués. Cependant, son fils Kenneth joue un rôle dirigeant majeur au sein de l'entreprise. Il occupe le poste de VP du conseil d'administration et dirige plusieurs de ses filiales."

#2420. Saint-Maximin affiche son bonheur

Désormais âgé de 29 ans, l'ancienne pépite stéphanoise Allan Saint-Maximin a accordé il y a 15 jours un entretien à The Athletic.

"Le Mexique était l'un des meilleurs endroits où j'ai pu vivre, et ma famille s'y sentait tellement bien. Mais parfois, des choses arrivent et vous n'avez pas le choix. Des choses se sont produites avec mes enfants, pas seulement une fois, mais à plusieurs reprises. Ils ont essayé de les harceler et leur ont dit des choses très blessantes à cause de leur couleur de peau. À cause de cela, c'est devenu un problème majeur. Il faut réagir, il faut prendre une décision et la mienne a été de partir, parce que je ne voulais pas que cela arrive et que mes enfants se disent : "Oh, nous devons accepter ça.

Mon fils de sept ans, Dayde, a été particulièrement affecté parce qu'il ne comprenait pas l'espagnol aussi bien que ses sœurs, Lyana et Ninhia, et ne comprenait pas pourquoi les gens se moquaient de lui. Il me disait : "Ils rient à cause de mes vêtements". Et il ajoutait : "Non, je ne veux plus porter ça". Quand j'ai compris que c'était à cause de sa couleur de peau, je lui ai dit : "Mon fils, ils ne parlaient pas de tes vêtements, ils parlaient de ta peau. Et ils faisaient des blagues parce que tu n'as pas la même peau qu'eux.

C'est douloureux. Certaines personnes ne voulaient pas jouer avec lui à cause de ça. "C'est comme ça dans tous les pays, ce n'est pas propre au Mexique. On ne peut pas accepter cela. Si cela s'était produit en France, en Arabie saoudite ou en Amérique, j'aurais pris exactement la même décision. Je devais taper du poing sur la table et prendre cette décision pour ma famille. Après une belle expérience de quelques mois à Lens, je suis heureux à Charlotte. Il n'y a pas de meilleur endroit que l'Amérique pour les enfants, c'est le rêve américain. À chaque fois que j'ouvre mon téléphone, je vois un but incroyable en MLS. Il y a tellement de buts spectaculaires dans cette ligue !

Le système actuel n'aime pas les joueurs comme moi. C'est un fait. Ils n'aiment pas les joueurs dotés d'une grande qualité de dribble et qui font se lever les supporters. Il faut trouver un équilibre, vous n'êtes pas là juste pour faire des passements de jambes et pénaliser votre équipe. Mais c'est très important de préserver cela. Rayan Cherki, qui évolue à Manchester City, est l'un des joueurs que je préfère regarder. Les supporters viennent au stade pour quoi ? Est-ce qu'ils viennent nous voir faire passe, passe, passe ? Ils ne viennent pas pour ça. Ils viennent pour un joueur comme Lamine Yamal, Kylian Mbappé, Ronaldo, Ronaldinho, Zidane...  viennent pour ce genre de joueur. À long terme, si nous continuons à perdre ce type de profil, le football va devenir tellement ennuyeux... Ce n'est pas ce que nous voulons.

Enfant, je jouais des petits matchs entre amis dans les rues de Meudon, près de Paris. Je jouais dehors tous les jours et c'est ce qui manque un peu aujourd'hui. On ne voit plus beaucoup de jeunes jouer dehors : ils jouent à la PlayStation, restent sur leur téléphone, au lieu d'aller dehors et de jouer au foot toute la journée. À cause de cela, beaucoup de choses commencent à changer. Ce n'est pas seulement à cause des entraîneurs.

Pour les fans de Premier League, je suis surtout connu pour mes quatre années passées à Newcastle United. J'ai eu des opportunités de quitter ce club plus tôt mais les supporters tes Magpies m'ont tout donné. C'est comme avec vos enfants. Vous préparez tout pour eux. Vous ramassez leur caca, vous nettoyez, vous faites tout ce que vous pouvez. Et quand ils arrivent au sommet, ils vous regardent en disant : "Merci papa, maintenant tu peux t'en aller, je vais vivre ma vie". Non, vous ne pouvez pas trahir comme ça. Ils m'ont tout donné et c'était très important d'être loyal.

Steve Bruce était un entraîneur incroyable, mais nous n'avions pas un effectif incroyable. Je n'oublierai jamais les supporters de Newcastle. Mon souvenir marquant, c'est le 3-3 contre le Manchester City de Pep Guardiola à St James' Park. J'avais délivré deux passes décisives. Quand vous jouez contre une grande équipe, que vous voyez Guardiola au bord du terrain, que vous prenez le ballon, et qu'il met sa main sur sa tête en mode "Mais c'est qui ce joueur ? C'est un cauchemar"..., vous sentez que quelque chose est différent. Entendre le soutien du public quand vous prenez le ballon, le sentir vous pousser... Vous avez l'impression de voler. C'est quelque chose de spécial.

Un autre moment marquant a été mon but victorieux contre Oxford United lors d'un match rejoué du 4e tour de la FA Cup, et l'incident de la célébration du "péniscoptère" qui a suivi. Alors que je célébrais mon but lors de ce match diffusé en direct sur la BBC, un supporter de Newcastle a été aperçu en train de s'exhiber dans la tribune extérieure. L'incident est devenu viral et a entraîné une enquête policière. C'était fou. C'était incroyable, c'était un match de coupe contre une équipe de League One (D3). Ce sont ce genre de choses qui vous aident à comprendre ce qu'est Newcastle.

En plus de mon académie, je m'occupe d'Helios, un jeu de cartes inspiré de la mythologie grecque que j'ai créé. J'ai récemment reçu offre de 26 millions d'euros de la part d'une entreprise pour racheter ce jeu gratuit mais je l'ai refusée car je souhaite continuer à le développer lui-même. Tout n'est pas une question d'argent. Le but est de créer quelque chose, de créer des emplois pour les gens, de créer des opportunités. Je préférerais m'associer avec d'autres personnes qui prendraient des parts dans la société en échange de leur investissement.

Alors que j'ai 29 ans et que je me prépare pour les play-offs de MLS avec Charlotte, quel conseil je donnerais au jeune homme que j'étais ? J'étais moins intelligent qu'aujourd'hui. C'est comme un jeu d'échecs, et j'aime jouer aux échecs. Il faut être malin et utiliser sa tête, pas seulement ses émotions. Je n'ai jamais joué en Ligue des champions mais j'estime que j'aurais mérité une sélection en équipe de France à un moment donné de ma carrière. Je suis heureux de tout ce que j'ai accompli. C'est le problème de l'être humain : toujours vouloir plus et ne pas vraiment apprécier ce qu'il a. Je vais continuer à travailler pour faire encore mieux, mais je suis heureux de ce que j'ai."

As part of PortuGOAL’s efforts to constantly evolve and serve lovers of Portuguese football with top-quality and increasingly accessible content, we are replacing the PortuGOAL Android and Apple APPs with a new Progressive Web Application (PWA) as from tomorrow, Tuesday 3 April.

#2417. Bosz frustrated as Feyenoord hold PSV to 1-1 draw in Eindhoven

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

Feyenoord preserved their four-point advantage over champions PSV after coming away with a point from Eindhoven thanks to an improved second-half performance.

PSV dominated the first half as they deployed a close-passing, tiki-taka style to stifle Feyenoord, but only had Guus Til’s well-taken volley after 20 minutes to show for it.

Ten minutes after the break Feyenoord equalised when Anis Hadj Moussa was given too much space to charge down the right flank and bend a shot inside the far post.

The Algerian had a good chance for a second towards the end, while PSV goalkeeper Matej Kovar had to be alert to keep out a header by Nacho Ferri from close range.

“We were by far the better team today and had the better chances,” said PSV coach Peter Bosz. “We started the second half badly and made a mistake, after which Hadj Moussa scored. And even then we could have won it.”

Ajax overtook PSV to close the gap at the top to three points with a hard-fought win at Sparta. Veteran striker Jens Toornstra scored for Sparta on his 500th Eredivisie appearance, but Abdellah Ouazane and Tolu Arokodare, the scorers against Atalanta in midweek, were on target again to claim the win for Ajax.

Twente moved up to fourth place as two late goals by Sondre Ørjasæter and Lucas Vennegoor of Hesselink earned a 2-0 win at AZ Alkmaar, who like Twente and PSV have 20 points.

Fortuna Sittard also snatched the points late on against Excelsior as Rodrigo Guth headed in a corner in injury time to keep the Limburg club in sixth place, two points further back.

At the other end of the table, the return of Adrie Koster as veteran coach brought no relief to Willem II, who crashed to a 6-1 home defeat to Cambuur.

The Tilburgers remain four points behind ADO Den Haag, who were unable to keep up the momentum of last week’s win against PSV as they lost 3-2 at home to Heerenveen.

Telstar lost by the same margin at Go Ahead Eagles, whose striker Søren Tengstedt netted twice in the first half to lead the Eredivisie scoring charts with eight goals.

#2416. Taylor, la Juve prepara 40 milioni a gennaio: la Lazio fa muro

Lotito e Fabiani hanno garantito a Gattuso che i giocatori più importanti non partiranno nella sessione invernale.

La Juventus prepara un'offerta da 40 milioni di euro per Kenneth Taylor a gennaio, ma la Lazio non vuole cederlo durante l'inverno. i bianconeri seguono il centrocampista olandese dall'estate e puntano ad anticipare la concorrenza inglese. Lotito e Fabiani hanno garantito a Gattuso che non partiranno giocatori importanti, soprattutto se il mercato in entrata resterà bloccato.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

La Lazio ha acquistato Taylor meno di dieci mesi fa per meno di 17 milioni di euro. Una cessione a 40 milioni produrrebbe una plusvalenza, anche tenendo conto del 10% che spetta ancora all'Ajax.

La linea del club sulle cessioni invernali è riassunta nella risposta «se ne parla a giugno», ribadita agli agenti che stanno presentando offerte per altri giocatori. Tra queste c'è una proposta del Brentford da 25 milioni per Isaksen. Per Taylor, Brighton e Bournemouth guardano a giugno, mentre la Juve vorrebbe muoversi già a gennaio.

Conta anche la volontà del calciatore. Taylor guadagna 1,7 milioni di euro e la Juventus gli offrirebbe il doppio. Il suo accordo prevede inoltre un premio fedeltà di 190mila euro per ogni sessione di mercato trascorsa alla Lazio: il primo assegno è arrivato a settembre, il secondo potrebbe arrivare a febbraio.

Il premio e l'attuale primo posto della Lazio sono incentivi a restare, ma Taylor, a 24 anni, ha grandi ambizioni e ha risentito dell'ultima esclusione dalla nazionale olandese. Il ct Xavi ha richiamato la concorrenza e i ritorni di Kluivert e De Jong, lasciando aperta la porta. Il colpo rimediato al Maradona non dovrebbe impedirgli di giocare sabato contro il Lecce, alla ripresa del campionato.

A Roma il centrocampista è cresciuto. Contro Cagliari e Napoli ha recuperato palloni, accompagnato le ripartenze e partecipato alla costruzione del gioco. Ha segnato il gol decisivo a Parma e, contro il Sassuolo, ha realizzato una rete e fornito un assist. Sa inserirsi, rifinire e contrastare; con Belahyane e Frattesi ha contribuito al funzionamento del centrocampo.

Pochi mesi fa sulla sua permanenza pesavano i primi sei mesi a Roma, l'addio di giocatori importanti, il mercato a saldo zero, le ambizioni ridimensionate e motivi personali e sentimentali. Poi sono arrivati le vittorie e la fiducia, e Taylor è diventato un leader della Lazio.

Gattuso ha espresso così la volontà di non perdere i suoi giocatori a gennaio: «Spero che a gennaio nessuno venga nel mio ufficio. Potrei chiudere la porta a chiave».

#2414. Sports club members becoming less tolerant of homosexuality

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

Acceptance of homosexuality in Dutch sports clubs has declined significantly in the last generation, according to a survey of attitudes by the Mulier Institute.

The organisation found that 66% of people had no objection to a fellow member of a sports club being gay, while in 2008 the figure was 85%.

Just over half (55%) of respondents said they had no problem sharing a shower with a gay team-mate of the same sex, which also represented a drop from 73% in 2008.

The Mulier Institute said the findings reflected a social trend that has also been seen in schools, where the proportion of boys aged between 12 and 16 with a positive view of homosexuality has dropped from 40% to 22% since 2021.

The Dutch Olympic Committee NOC*NSF said the findings were “highly regrettable” and pledged to step up its efforts to make sport more inclusive.

But the John Blankenstein Foundation, which campaigns for LGBT emancipation in sport, said it had noticed declining enthusiasm for its workshops and growing numbers of parents who voiced objections.

Karin Blankenstein, who set up the organisation in 2008, told NOS: “I get the sensitivity around transgender people in sport at the top level, but this is about amateur sport.”

#2412. Top five football betting tips and tricks

Football is the world’s most famous sport, and it is the sport of choice of a great many sports punters as well. Especially popular in Europe, betting on football can be an incredibly profitable enterprise, so long as you know how to do it.

Getting started on football betting is far from easy, though. This form of gambling requires dedication, investment, and persistence. As you gain experience, your intuition will improve and you will be able to make more correct predictions.

But experience is not the only thing that can help you become a better football punter. In fact, you can substantially improve your betting strategy by simply following some basic football betting tips.

You might find shopping around boring and unhelpful, but you just have to do it when you search sites for football betting. As you know, the higher the odds, the bigger the payout. What you may have missed, however, is that odds are different at each bookmaker. Having said that, shopping around for the best odds is sensible, as it rewards you with bigger payouts.

In this line of thought, sticking to one bookmaker is virtually pointless. Even if the bookie of your choosing offers the best odds for a number of matches, it is highly unlikely that their odds will remain the best for long.

An obvious one, we know. And yet, you will be surprised by the number of football bettors who wager on football, but do not follow the sport. So, why is it important to follow the action closely?

First of all, by watching football matches, you can take part in the most exciting football betting market – the live betting market. What is more important, however, is that following the action provides an invaluable insight into teams and players’ style, condition, and determination – important factors  to consider when placing a bet.

As you watch more and more games, you will improve your analytic abilities. In time, you will be able to make more accurate predictions about the outcome of different matches, which, of course,  will result in placing more winning bets.

As you know, odds are based on the probability of occurrence of a certain outcome; the higher the odds, the more unlikely it is for that outcome to actually occur. But what happens when the odds offered by the bookmaker represent a probability that is lower than the actual probability of an outcome occurring? In such a situation, you can place what is called a value bet.

If you want to become a professional football punter, you should always search for value when betting, as it leads to huge profits

Favoured by beginners in particular, accumulators are a type of football wager which allows you to bet on as many fixtures as you like. Most commonly, you will be asked to choose a match winner, but as the market is diverse, you may be offered a number of other betting options. What makes accumulators an appealing betting market is the huge potential payout – here, you can stake as little as £1 and have the potential to win more than £500,000, depending on the bookmaker and the number of selected fixtures.

If you find accumulators’ payouts too good to be true, you are right. As a matter of fact, there is a reason for the odds to be set so high – actually winning an accumulator bet, especially one which involves a wide selection of matches, is nearly impossible. You see, all you need for your whole wager to lose is one wrong prediction. For example, if you bet on 10 matches and just one of them goes wrong, your entire accumulator is lost.

To make money by betting on football, avoid betting on accumulators – the risk is simply not worth it.

Finally, if you want to be a successful football punter, learn how not to mix emotions with betting. This is easier said than done, though.

Whenever your favourite team plays, you want them to win, regardless of how (im)possible that may be. If you do not learn how to leave emotions out of betting, however, you are highly likely to put money on your favourite team even if they have no chance of winning the match. Conversely, should you base your bets on hard facts and not on emotions, you increase your chances to win.

Our advice – avoid betting on fixtures that involve one of your favourite teams, that is the easiest way to keep emotions at bay.

#2411. Onorato: “Minacce a Lotito intollerabili. Ma faccia uno sforzo verso i tifosi”

L'assessore difende Lotito dalle intimidazioni, chiede un gesto verso i tifosi e fa il punto sul progetto Flaminio.

Lazio, Onorato difende Lotito ma apre ai tifosi: “Faccia un gesto di distensione”

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Solidarietà a Claudio Lotito per le intimidazioni ricevute, ma anche un invito al presidente della Lazio a compiere un passo verso quella parte della tifoseria che si sente tradita. Alessandro Onorato interviene nel momento delicato del rapporto tra il patron biancoceleste e i sostenitori laziali.

L'assessore allo Sport di Roma Capitale parla anche dello sciopero del tifo e rivela di continuare ad andare allo stadio insieme alle figlie. Sullo sfondo c'è poi un altro tema centrale per il futuro della Lazio: il Flaminio. Secondo Onorato, l'iter potrebbe arrivare alla dichiarazione di interesse pubblico entro Natale, a determinate condizioni.

Secondo quanto riportato da Il Foglio, Onorato ha affrontato i diversi temi partendo proprio dalle intimidazioni indirizzate a Lotito.

“Le intimidazioni a Lotito? Intollerabili e ingiustificabili, persino con minacce di morte al figlio, gli sono solidale e penso faccia bene ad avere il coraggio di andare contro chi utilizza la curva per fare pressioni indebite. Lo sciopero del tifo? Quando ho tempo, io allo stadio ci vado insieme alle mie figlie: non vorrei che perdessero la passione per il biancazzurro, penso però che il presidente debba fare uno sforzo verso centinaia di migliaia di tifosi per bene”.

Onorato non intende entrare direttamente nella polemica calcistica tra Claudio Lotito e i tifosi della Lazio.

“Da uomo delle istituzioni farei una cosa inopportuna”.

L'assessore sottolinea però un elemento particolare del momento attraversato dal club:

“È un peccato che ci sia questa protesta proprio ora che la Lazio è prima in classifica”.

Onorato distingue inoltre la posizione degli ultrà da quella di altri sostenitori che hanno scelto di non andare allo stadio:

“C’è anche tanta gente, non ultrà, che non va allo stadio perché si sente tradita da Lotito. Verso queste persone sarebbe bello se il presidente facesse un gesto di distensione. La stragrande maggioranza delle persone sane che amano la Lazio in questo momento sta soffrendo. È evidente che serve anche alla società distinguere tra il tifo malato e quello sano”.

Nella protesta è intervenuto anche il presidente della Regione Lazio Francesco Rocca. Una scelta che Onorato non avrebbe compiuto:

“Fossi stato in lui non sarei entrato in questa polemica. Penso sia una questione interna alla destra che però danneggia la comunità del tifo laziale”.

Allo stesso tempo, l'assessore riconosce a Lotito un merito preciso risalente all'inizio della sua presidenza:

“Lotito ha avuto il grande merito, sin dall’inizio della sua presidenza alla Lazio, nel 2004, di distinguere con nettezza la parte sana del tifo dalle infiltrazioni criminali. Anche per questo sarebbe bello che oggi tendesse una mano verso i primi per superare un clima che fa solo male alla squadra: un calcio senza tifosi è davvero triste”.

Non si tratta, secondo Onorato, di una difesa politica del senatore di Forza Italia. Il problema avrebbe infatti dimensioni molto più ampie.

“Non è questo. Non è un problema che riguarda solo la Lazio. Mi chiedo perché il governo Meloni non faccia qualcosa di veramente incisivo per intervenire su tantissimi gruppi ultrà che hanno interessi illeciti e tendono a fare pressioni indebite sulle presidenze. Non solo su Lotito, ma penso a quello che è avvenuto al Lecce, all’Inter, al Milan, alla Juve o in altri comuni con squadre più piccole. Avere il coraggio di prendere le distanze da questi gruppi organizzati nel calcio di oggi è molto complesso. Anche perché le contiguità con la destra sono note”.

Onorato torna anche su una delle prime decisioni adottate da Lotito da presidente della Lazio, quando tolse il merchandising agli Irriducibili.

“E fece benissimo. Se vogliamo un calcio all’altezza delle sfide internazionali, il merchandising deve essere appannaggio delle società. È una voce importante che consente ai club di realizzare ricavi che possono poi essere reinvestiti nella crescita della squadra”.

Altro capitolo importante riguarda il nuovo stadio della Lazio e il progetto di riqualificazione del Flaminio.

In questo caso Onorato mette da parte qualsiasi appartenenza politica o sportiva:

“Su questo non ci sono colori politici, né sportivi: faccio quello che mi spetta da assessore”.

L'assessore giudica positivamente il lavoro presentato dalla Lazio e spiega a che punto si trova il procedimento:

“Posso però senz’altro dire che il progetto è molto avanzato e, a differenza di quanto detto da tanti, è un progetto serio. Si è conclusa la prima conferenza dei servizi, con alcuni rilievi della Sovrintendenza statale, ai quali la Lazio ha risposto. Adesso stiamo aspettando che risponda a sua volta il ministero per poter completare l’iter”.

I prossimi passaggi potrebbero risultare decisivi e Onorato indica anche una possibile tempistica:

“Se nei prossimi giorni la Sovrintendenza risponde e la Lazio adegua il progetto e il piano economico-finanziario, noi entro Natale possiamo chiudere l’iter per arrivare alla dichiarazione di interesse pubblico”.

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

A late equaliser for Fortuna Sittard in De Kuip proved costly for Feyenoord as PSV and Twente racked up huge victories to move within two points of the Eredivisie leaders.

Feyenoord paid the price for failing to finish off the Limburgers, with Luciano Valente’s 59th-minute goal the only reward for their dominance.

Fortuna equalised with four minutes left when Shiloh ’t Zand, on loan from Feyenoord, was allowed to run through to the penalty area and beat Tjark Ernst with a low shot.

Feyenoord coach Giovanni van Bronckhorst made no effort to hide his disappointment. “We know that Fortuna are the kind of team who can score from the few attacks they have,” he said. “If you don’t score the goals, things like this can happen.”

Second-placed Ajax are a point behind after a narrow win at home against AZ ended an 11-match winless streak against the Alkmaar club, as Tolu Arokodare’s early header proved decisive.

Twente recorded the biggest win of the weekend with a 9-0 demolition of Excelsior in Rotterdam, with Wout Weghorst scoring four times and Sondre Ørjasaeter notching up five assists.

Excelsior collapsed following the fifth-minute dismissal of Nesto Groen for a reckless high challenge. The home side were 5-0 down at half-time, with Weghorst claiming a hat-trick in the opening 33 minutes.

PSV bagged seven goals in a one-sided encounter with bottom club Willem II, with all the goals coming from different scorers including Ricardo Pepi, who came on in the second half after a six-week absence through injury.

Telstar opened up a three-point gap at the bottom over ADO Den Haag with a 4-2 win against Utrecht in a painful return to Velsen-Zuid for Telstar’s former coach Anthony Correia.

ADO went down 1-0 in Zwolle, with PEC’s Dylan Mbayo scoring the only goal with a first-time finish on the stroke of half-time.

The match between Cambuur Leeuwarden and Go Ahead Eagles was suspended in the 82nd minute after visiting fans threw fireworks onto the pitch.

The clubs agreed with the KNVB not to play the final minutes and allow Cambuur’s 4-0 advantage to stand as the final result.

Go Ahead’s director Jan-Willem van Dop said the club would take action against those responsible: “I am ashamed on behalf a small group of supporters who ruined things for the club and a large hare of the fans in the away end,” he said.
